#### Unit 1 — Powers, Exponents and Roots (opener printed 1)

- 1-1 Powers and Exponents, printed 2–7. Repeated multiplication;
  exponent, power and base (English vocabulary glosses are given in the
  lesson sidebars); the general form a×a×…×a (n factors) = aⁿ. Get
  Ready story: Euglena single-cell division doubling (1, 2, 4, …; how
  many cells after 8 divisions?). Think & Discuss: 2⁷×2⁵, with three
  pupil answers (2¹², 2³⁵, 4³⁵) to judge. Then the multiplication and
  division laws for powers with the same base; the zero exponent; negative
  exponents; factor trees (625 = 5⁴, 72 = 2³×3²); evaluating expressions
  at a = 2, b = −5; a technology example in bytes (2³⁰ bytes, about a
  gigabyte). Lesson Assessment printed 6–7, including a Sun-mass problem
  in scientific-notation form.
- 1-2 Scientific Notation, printed 8–12. Writing very large and very
  small numbers in scientific notation (a×10ⁿ, 1 ≤ |a| < 10); converting
  to standard form; ordering numbers in scientific notation; operations
  on numbers in scientific notation. Get Ready: the Earth–Sun distance,
  149,600,000 km, written 1.496×10⁸ km, reached two ways. Worked contexts:
  planet masses ordered (Earth, Venus, Mercury, Mars); the Sun's core
  temperature (15 million degrees); a hydrogen atom's mass; a bee's
  wingbeats (about 230 per second); ocean areas in a table. Lesson
  Assessment printed 11–12.
- 1-3 Square Roots and Cube Roots, printed 13–20. Square arrays and cube
  arrays (144 small squares; 729 small cubes); perfect squares and
  perfect cubes; the square root of a perfect square having two roots, ±;
  √a² = |a|; solving equations of the form x² = a and x³ = a. Worked
  contexts: a 400 m² square garden (side 20 m, fence 80 m); a cube of
  volume 512 (edge 8, one face 64, lateral area 256, total area 384).
  Lesson Assessment printed 17–18.
- Unit One Assessment, printed 19; Unit One Activity printed 19: "Planets
  of the Solar System" (an AI-assisted task: gather planet data, tabulate
  it, write masses in scientific notation).

#### Unit 2 — Algebra (opener printed 20)

- 2-1 Inequalities, printed 21–26. The inequality concept and symbols
  (less than, greater than, less than or equals, greater than or equals);
  first-degree inequalities in one variable; writing inequalities from
  situations (driving-licence age 18 or over; a minimum phone-use age
  of 12);
  solving by the addition, subtraction, multiplication and division
  properties, including the sign flip when multiplying or dividing by a
  negative; solution sets in N, Z and Q; verification by substitution; a
  "spot the mistake" item (two pupils, Mustafa and Salma, solving
  2−3x < 17 in Z). Word problems:
  pens (5x + 20 ≤ 150, so x ≤ 26); a basketball scorer (Marwan, 12 points
  or more); Hamza saving (250 a week target, 100 in hand, 20 a week);
  Zeinab shopping (a 240 shirt, a 400 discount card, a budget not
  exceeding 3200).
  Lesson Assessment printed 25–26.
- 2-2 Multiplying an Algebraic Term by an Algebraic Term or Expression,
  printed 27–31. Term-by-term multiplication (a^m × b^n, coefficients
  multiplied, exponents added); the distributive law a(b + c) and
  a(b − c); the vertical method; area models. Worked contexts: a tennis
  court (area expression from its length and width); a T-shaped paper
  cut-out; a swimming-pool surround (4x(5x + 8) minus the pool area);
  a water tank volume problem solved two ways (x³ = 27, so x = 3).
  Lesson Assessment printed 30–31.
- 2-3 Multiplying Algebraic Expressions, printed 32–37. Binomial-by-
  binomial multiplication, (x + a)(x + b) = x² + (a + b)x + ab, via the
  area model, the "multiply by sight" method and the vertical method;
  special cases: (a + b)², (a − b)² and (a + b)(a − b) = a² − b²;
  binomial-by-trinomial multiplication including the vertical method.
  Worked contexts: Omar's garden expansion (length +7, width +3);
  a rug on a floor (2x + 6 by 2x + 4, minus a 6 × 4 rug); a swimming pool
  10 by 8 with an x-wide border; a flower bed 15 by 6 with a border;
  a shaded-area expression. Creative item: a square whose side grows by
  2 and whose area grows by 24 — find the original side. Lesson
  Assessment printed 36–37.
- 2-4 Dividing an Algebraic Term or Expression by an Algebraic Term,
  printed 38–42. Term-by-term division (divide coefficients, subtract
  exponents; division by zero impossible); expression-by-term division,
  term by term. Worked contexts: Luxor's Avenue of Sphinxes (area
  7xy² + 11x³y type expressions); a triangle of area 15x⁴ + 6x³ + 9x²
  with height 3x² (base 10x² + 4x + 6, and 108 at x = 3); garden beds;
  a rectangular garden (15a³b² + 20a²b³ − 25a²b²) of length 5a²b²;
  fuel consumption (9x³ + 18x² + 27x); class counts; a lawn area;
  rectangles of volume 24x³ + 32x² on a square side 2x. "Spot the
  mistake": two pupils dividing (4x² − 6x) by −2x. Lesson Assessment
  printed 41–42.
- 2-5 Dividing Algebraic Expressions, printed 43–47. Division as the
  inverse of multiplication; long division layout, including the missing-
  term note (write a term of coefficient zero for an absent power);
  finding the other factor when one factor is known; remainder problems
  (for example (x³ + x² + a) divided by (x − 2) with a = −12). Worked
  contexts: the Bibliotheca Alexandrina (shelves and books); a land
  plot of area x² + 7x + 12 and width x + 3; an apartment of area
  x³ + 15x² + 51x + 10 and width x + 10 (length x² + 5x + 1, perimeter
  54 m at x = 2); a shopkeeper's revenues. Lesson Assessment printed
  46–47.
- Unit Two Assessment, printed 48; Unit Two Activity printed 48: research
  the FOIL method online (teams of 2–4 pupils; compare FOIL with the
  vertical method; collect the mathematicians behind algebraic terms).

#### Unit 3 — Geometry and Measurement (opener printed 49)

- 3-1 Areas, printed 50–55. Review of square, rectangle, parallelogram
  and triangle area and perimeter formulas; the rhombus by side and
  height and by diagonals (A = ½d₁×d₂: diagonals 5 and 8 give 20; area 96
  with one diagonal 12 gives the other 16); the square by its diagonal
  (A = ½d²: diagonal 6 gives 18; a 12-diagonal square, 72, against an
  11 × 7 rectangle, 77); the trapezium (A = ½(b₁ + b₂)×h) and its midline
  (half the sum of the bases: (6 + 10) × 6 / 2 = 48; (5 + 15) × 7.5 / 2
  = 75; area 54 with height 9 and small base 4 gives large base 8; area
  45 with height 5 gives midline 9). Get Ready: a map of Aswan
  governorate (74 km, 388 km, 250 km) asking its area. Lesson Assessment
  printed 54–55, including a rhombus-diagonal farm plot and two plots of
  equal area (27 m²).
- 3-2 Geometric Constructions, printed 56–60. Bisecting an angle with
  ruler and compass (step-by-step diagrams); bisecting a line segment
  (AB, with AE = EB); drawing a triangle from three sides (a 3–4–5
  triangle); from two sides and the included angle (AB 4, AC 3,
  angle A 65°); from a side and two angles (AB 4, angles 50° and 45°);
  the right-triangle median theorem in construction form (AB 4, BC 3,
  right angle at B, D the midpoint of AC, BD = ½AC = 2.5). Lesson
  Assessment printed 59–60, including a medal-design activity and
  drawing a triangle's angle bisectors (what do you notice?).
- 3-3 Geometric Transformations, printed 61–68. Transformation, reflection,
  translation and rotation defined; centre of rotation; clockwise and
  anticlockwise; the image congruent to the original. Reflection in the
  x-axis (A(x, y) → (x, −y)) and y-axis (A(x, y) → (−x, y)), worked on a
  triangle with vertices (2, 0), (4, 1), (1, 3); translation by (a, b)
  (A(x, y) → (x + a, y + b)); rotation about the origin R(O, 90°)
  ((x, y) → (−y, x)), R(O, −90°) ((x, y) → (y, −x)) and R(O, ±180°)
  ((x, y) → (−x, −y)), with R(O, 90°) identified with R(O, −270°); a
  GeoGebra activity; a starfish note (72° rotational symmetry);
  properties preserved (lengths, angle measures, order, orientation
  notes). Get Ready: a butterfly's mirror wings; Think & Discuss: the
  reversed "ambulance" writing seen in a mirror. Lesson Assessment
  printed 66–68, including a 20-seat Ferris wheel (angle per seat) and a
  "25" digits reflection puzzle.
- 3-4 Composition of Geometric Transformations, printed 69–73. A composite
  as a sequence of transformations; an equivalent single transformation;
  vocabulary: Composite, Equivalent. Worked composites: reflection in x
  then in y; two translations; R(O, 90°) then R(O, 180°); equivalences
  (reflection in x followed by reflection in y equals rotation by 180°;
  translations add). Get Ready: an Islamic ornament dome; Think &
  Discuss: a swan and its reflection. Lesson Assessment printed 71–73,
  including two pupils' competing translation orders and a creative
  transformation (x, y) → (−y, x − 2).
- Unit Three Assessment, printed 74; Unit Three Activity printed 74:
  "Geometric Constructions and Décor" (choosing a wall spot and hanging
  a picture with constructions).

#### Unit 4 — Probability (opener printed 75)

- 4-1 Random Experiment – Sample Space – Events, printed 76–81. The
  random experiment (all outcomes known beforehand, the actual outcome
  not predictable); sample space S and its size n(S); events: simple,
  certain (S), impossible (φ), compound. Worked sample spaces: one coin
  (S = {H, T}, n(S) = 2); two coins (S = {(H,H), (H,T), (T,H), (T,T)},
  n(S) = 4, with tree diagram); two dice (6 × 6 = 36, with table and
  lattice diagram); a die (S = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}) with events such as
  even (A = {2, 4, 6}), greater than 1 (B = {2, 3, 4, 5, 6}), exactly 2
  (C = {2}), certain (D = S) and impossible (E = φ); two-digit numbers
  from digits {3, 4, 6, 7}. Get Ready: a school activities table (art
  versus sport: music, drawing, football, volleyball, basketball).
  Think & Discuss: blind ball draws (three red balls; known versus
  unknown colours). Lesson Assessment printed 80–81, including a card
  number 5742, four coin tosses, dice events, cards 1–25, a youth-centre
  activities sample space and a restaurant menu combination count.
- 4-2 Theoretical Probability and Experimental Probability, printed
  82–87. Theoretical probability P(A) = favourable outcomes / total
  outcomes, with P(φ) = 0, P(S) = 1 and 0 ≤ P ≤ 1 on a scale; probability
  written as a fraction, a decimal or a percentage. Worked examples: a
  die (even 3/6 = 1/2; less than 8, 6/6 = 1; prime 2/6 = 1/3; exactly 4,
  1/6; greater than 6, 0); two coins (two heads 1/4; at least one head
  3/4; same face 2/4 = 1/2; head on the first toss 2/4); a bag of 1 red,
  6 blue and 3 green balls (P(blue) = 0.6, P(white) = 0, P(red) = 0.1,
  P(green) = 0.3, P(blue or green) = 0.9, P(not green) = 0.7, with the
  complement method). Experimental probability as observed count over
  trial count: a coin tossed 100 times with heads 41 times (0.41 against
  the theoretical 0.5, with the note that more trials bring the two
  closer); a five-colour spinner spun 50 times (yellow 13 times, 0.26,
  against the theoretical 1/5 = 0.20); a die rolled 150 times with a
  results table (28, 19, 23, 28, 25, 27). Get Ready: the Egypt football
  team's penalty kicks (12 scored from 15; a second player 9 from 15 —
  who should the coach pick?). Think & Discuss: three spinners (Mohamed,
  Mariam, Maged) and equal chances. Lesson Assessment printed 86–87,
  including pens (5 red, 2 blue and 3 black; P(blue) = 1/5), dice events, a 9-section spinner,
  a 15-pupil class by hair colour, cards 20–29, digits {3, 4, 5, 7} as
  two-digit numbers, a library (12 stories, 8 history, 10 science),
  two basketball players (15 throws with 6 scored against 20 throws with
  9 scored), a sports-preference bar chart for 1000 pupils, a homework-
  hours stem-and-leaf plot for 30 pupils, and a bag with P(red) = 2/3
  and 5 white balls (find the total).
- Unit Four Assessment, printed 88–89; Unit Four Activity printed 89:
  "Who's the Likely Winner" (a spinner game for four players, recording
  results and comparing theoretical with experimental probability, and
  judging whether the game is fair).
- Final Assessment, printed 90 (a single page).

#### Unit 1 — Numbers and Their Operations (opener printed 1)

- 1-1 Properties of Proportion, printed 2–7. Proportion a/b = c/d with a
  the first proportional, b the second, c the third and d the fourth; a
  and d the extremes, b and c the means; cross multiplication. Checks:
  4/6 = 10/15 is proportional, 2/7 ≠ 4/21 is not. The fourth proportional
  (8, 12, 14 give x = 21); finding a from 3, a, 15, 20 (a = ±10). The
  proportion properties proved with the k-substitution (a = bk, c = dk):
  invertendo, alternendo, componendo and dividendo forms such as
  (a + b)/(a − b) = (c + d)/(c − d); the equal-ratios theorem (a/b = c/d
  = e/f = k gives (a + c + e)/(b + d + f) = k) and its weighted form
  ((am + cn + el)/(bm + dn + fl) = k). Worked examples: x/a = y/6 =
  (4x − 3y)/10 giving a = 7; 2a = 3b = 4c with a + b + c = 52 giving
  a = 24, b = 16, c = 12. Get Ready story: a Tutankhamun documentary
  (12,000 views on one platform in 4 hours against 18,000 on a second in
  6 hours — are the rates equal?; combined views per hour). Think &
  Discuss: triangle ABC enlarged to DEF by a ratio, find the perimeter
  of ABC. Vocabulary sidebars give the English glosses (Proportion,
  First/Second/Third/Fourth proportional, Extremes, Means, Cross
  multiplication). Lesson Assessment printed 6–7, including a triangle
  of perimeter 61 whose sides, after adjustments of −8, −4 and +15, fall
  in ratio 3:5:8 (find AB), a computer-games points
  problem (581 points) and a triangular flower bed with proportional
  sides.
- 1-2 Continued Proportion, printed 8–13. Continued proportion a/b = b/c;
  b the mean proportional, b² = ac, b = ±√(ac); the third proportional
  (6 and 18 give 54); the mean proportional of 15 and 135 (±45) and of
  3(x + y)² and 12(x − y)² (±6(x² − y²)); the generalisation to longer
  chains (a/b = b/c = c/d = … = k, giving a = ck² and, for four terms,
  a = dk³, b = dk², c = dk); the identity (a² + c²)/(b² + d²) = ac/bd
  proved by k-substitution; a triangle-sides problem (10 − x, 7 − x,
  5 − x in continued proportion gives x = 1 cm to subtract). Get Ready:
  the Egyptian Museum (an advertising cost of 25,000 and the number of
  advertisements). Think & Discuss: a 19 cm ribbon cut into three
  proportional lengths with the largest 9 cm. Worked equation case: x,
  y and 18 in continued proportion with x + y = 20 gives (x, y) = (8, 12)
  or (50, −30) — the negative solution is kept in the worked example.
  Lesson Assessment printed 12–13, including a flag-design activity
  (three parts, the middle 80 cm², total 200 cm²).
- Unit One Assessment, printed 14; Unit One Activity printed 14:
  "Proportion in Our Lives" (groups of three; price and quantity tables;
  is this class proportional to the first class?).

#### Unit 2 — Algebra (opener printed 15)

- 2-1 Cartesian Product, printed 16–21. The ordered pair (a, b) with
  first and second projections; equality of ordered pairs; X × Y as the
  set of ordered pairs; arrow diagrams and Cartesian diagrams; X²;
  n(X × Y) = n(X) × n(Y) and n(X²) = n(X)²; products with unions,
  intersections and differences ((X ∪ Y) × Z, (X ∩ Y) × Y,
  (X − Y) × (Y − X)); infinite products N × N, Z × Z, Q × Q and R × R
  (the coordinate plane). Get Ready: chess (64 squares, naming a square
  precisely). Think & Discuss: phone colours and storage options (3 × 3
  = 9 choices). A René Descartes note (1596–1650). Worked examples:
  (x, y − 2) = (3, 3); (x + 3, xy) = (5, −8); (3x², y³/2) = (12, −32);
  (√x, y²/5) = (3, 4); X = {2, 3} and Y = {1, 4, 5}. Lesson Assessment
  printed 20–21, including which product (1/2, √5) belongs to; a car
  number-plate count ({1, 3, 5, 7} × {a, b, c}); a probability item (a
  random pair from X × X with X = {1, …, 10}, the chance it lies in
  {1, 2, 3} × {1, 2, 3, 4}); and a counting item (X = {1, 2, 3},
  Y = {a, b, c}, pairs with an odd first projection and second
  projection b).
- 2-2 Relation and Function, printed 22–29. A relation R as a subset of
  X × Y; domain (first projections), range (second projections) and
  codomain; four representations (set of pairs, table, arrow diagram,
  graph); relations on a set (R ⊂ X × X, for example y = x − 2). A
  function: every domain element linked to exactly one element; its
  domain, codomain and range; function tests (an element with no arrow,
  an element with two arrows, a repeated first projection, the vertical-
  line test at x = 3). Symbolic notation f: X → Y, y = f(x), independent
  and dependent variables. Worked examples: y = x² on X = {1, 2, 3} into
  Y = {0, 1, 4, 9, 16} (domain {1, 2, 3}, codomain Y, range {1, 4, 9});
  y = 2^x on X = {2, 3, 4} into Y = {2, 4, 8, 12, 16} (R = {(2, 4),
  (3, 8), (4, 16)}); f(x) = x² − 3 (f(0) − f(1/3) = −1/9) and
  2f(√5) + 5f(√2) = −1; f(x) = 3 − 2x tabulated at x = −2, −1, 0, 1, 2
  (range {7, 5, 3, 1, −1}). Get Ready: the Arab League's 22 states (X =
  country names, Y = capitals; is the set of pairs a subset of X × Y?).
  Lesson Assessment printed 27–29, including relations given by seat
  numbers and students, mosque minarets and mosques, governorates and
  capitals, students and school activities; and a creative item (X =
  {x ∈ N, 10 ≤ x < 100}, y = 5x, count the relation's elements).
- 2-3 Linear Function, printed 30–35. The linear function f(x) = ax + b
  (a ≠ 0), first degree, its graph a straight line, intercepts (0, b)
  and (−b/a, 0); graphing from three points (f(x) = 6 − 2x tabulated at
  x = 0, 1, 2; g(x) = (1/3)x); the constant function f(x) = b (a
  horizontal line, zero degree; f(x) = 0 is the x-axis, not a linear
  function); applications with discrete domains noted (shirt production
  cost f(x) = 50x + 100: cost 700 gives x = 12, and x cannot be negative
  or fractional; a car distance d(t) = 100t + 50: 550 gives t = 5 hours).
  Get Ready: the Muhammad Ali Mosque at the Salah El-Din Citadel (120
  visitors at 10 a.m., 40 more an hour, f(t) = 120 + 40t; when 360
  visitors?). A GeoGebra activity. Lesson Assessment printed 34–35,
  including graph identification for f(x) = 3x − 4; a copier depreciating
  as f(t) = 12000 − 750t (value after 4 years; when 3000); a football-
  field rental f(t) = 120t + 80 (cost for 2 hours; when 680); supermarket
  baskets f(n) = 3n + 20 (height for 10 baskets; height 2 m gives n);
  and a creative item (the distance between the parallel lines f(x) = −3
  and g(x) = 4 is 7).
- 2-4 First-Degree Equations and Inequalities in One Variable, printed
  36–42. The general equation form ax + b = 0 (a ≠ 0) with non-examples
  (2√x − 0.5 = 0; x² − 1 = 3; 1/x + 4 = x − 3); solving in R (6x + 2 =
  4x − 4 gives x = −3; 5(√3x − 7) + 11 = 6 gives x = 2√3); the equality
  properties. The general inequality form ax + b < 0 (and >, ≤, ≥) with
  non-examples (x² − 3x ≥ −2; 1/y − 4 < 5; 2√x + 1 > 5); solving with
  number-line graphs (5x + 2 > 12 gives x > 2, ]2, ∞[; 5x − 3 ≤ 12 gives
  ]−∞, 3]; 2x − 3 ≤ 5x + 9 gives x ≥ −4, [−4, ∞[; 2(3x − 7) < 4x − 11
  gives x < 3/2); compound inequalities a < x < b split into two and
  solved by intersecting solution sets (−1 < x − 3 < 2 gives ]2, 5[;
  −8 < 3x − 2 ≤ 7 gives ]−2, 3]; −3 ≤ 3(x − 7) − 3 ≤ 6 gives [7, 10];
  4x − 1 < 3(x − 1) ≤ 4x + 4 gives [−7, −2[; 2x − 19 ≤ 3(x − 6) < x − 8
  gives [−1, 5[); geometric applications (triangle sides 9, 13 and
  2x − 6 give 5 < x < 14; a reflex angle (6x + 12)° gives an x range).
  Get Ready: a gym membership (100 joining fee, 20 per session, a 300
  monthly budget — how many days?). Think & Discuss: a square of side
  2x against a rectangle, equal perimeters. Lesson Assessment printed
  41–42, including two car-rental companies (900 a day + 1.5 per km
  against 1100 a day + 1 per km — the maximum distance for the first to
  stay cheaper); a triangle with sides 9, 3x + 5 and 20; and a rectangle
  (2x − 4) by 10 with perimeter 52, over 40, and area under 80.
- Unit Two Assessment, printed 43; Unit Two Activity printed 43: "Class
  Schedule and Ordered Pairs" (survey classmates' favourite subjects and
  days; build ordered-pair tables; compare answers).

#### Unit 3 — Geometry (opener printed 44)

- 3-1 Axioms of Inequality, printed 45–49. The lesson's own heading gloss
  is "Axioms of Inequality". An axiom defined as a statement assumed true
  and used as a basis, with Euclid's axioms as the example. The axiom
  table: addition (a < b gives a + c < b + c); subtraction; multiplication
  by a positive number; transitivity; sum (a < b and c < d give
  a + c < b + d); whole-part comparison (a = b + c with c > 0 gives
  a > b). Notes: the smaller angle's supplement exceeds the larger
  angle's supplement, and likewise for complements; an exterior angle
  exceeds either non-adjacent interior angle; the triangle inequality in
  the form |AC − AB| < BC < AC + AB. Proofs: AB = AC gives
  m(∠ABD) > m(∠ACD); an interior point D gives m(∠BDC) > m(∠A); a
  perpendicular-bisector and angle-bisector configuration giving
  m(∠3) < m(∠4). Get Ready: billiards (a white ball striking the blue,
  path areas ΔEAB against ΔEDC). Think & Discuss: m(∠BAC) = m(∠BDC)
  and m(∠ADC) > m(∠BAD) — compare m(∠BDA) with m(∠CAD). Lesson
  Assessment printed 48–49, including a parallelogram's diagonals
  (MC against MD); angle orderings; exterior-angle proofs; a school–
  hospital–home triangle shortest path; and possible values for AD and
  AC given 5.5, 12.5 and 24.
- 3-2 Inequality in Triangles, printed 50–56. The theorem: in a triangle,
  the longer side lies opposite the larger angle (proved with a point D
  on AB, AD = AC, using the isosceles triangle and the exterior angle);
  its converse; the hinge theorem (two triangles with two sides equal
  and the included angle different have different third sides) and its
  converse. Worked examples: ordering sides and angles; x ranges (a
  triangle with sides x + 12, 2x − 6 and 15 and m(∠A) < m(∠B) gives
  3 < x < 18; an angle (2x − 9)° case gives 4.5 < x < 17; a second case
  gives 3 < x < 6). Get Ready: bird migration (a triangle of 27, 21 and
  24 km — which angle is largest?). Think & Discuss: a 15-link chain
  formed into a triangle with different sides and m(∠B) > m(∠A) — the
  link counts for CB and AC. Lesson Assessment printed 54–56, including
  ordering angles and sides; comparisons; x ranges; a parallelogram
  diagonal proof; an ancient-tools angle question; a road junction
  (50° and 45°) asking which route is nearer C; and a right-triangle
  bisector proof.
- 3-3 Perpendicular Projections and Euclid's Theorem, printed 57–63. The
  projection of a point on a line (the foot of the perpendicular); the
  projection of a segment on a line (its endpoints' projections; a
  perpendicular segment projects to a point); right-triangle projections
  found via Pythagoras (AC = 15 with altitude 12 gives DC = 9 and
  DB = 5). Euclid's theorem: (AC)² = CD × CB and (AB)² = BD × BC, with
  the corollary (AD)² = DB × DC, AD the geometric mean of DB and DC,
  and AD = AB × AC / BC. A Euclid note (Greek mathematician, about
  300 BC, the Elements). Worked examples: BC = 4 from CD = 2 and
  CA = 2 + 6; SP from (2√7)² = SR × 7 giving SR = 4; XY from
  20² = 16(16 + ZM) giving ZM = 9; a rectangle ABCD with AF ⊥ BD
  (AE = 8, EF = 2 giving BE = 4, ED = 16, BD = 20); a rock's height, 17 m
  (a 1.7 m man with a 5.1 m shadow giving BD = 15.3). Get Ready: the
  Ramses II statue at the Grand Egyptian Museum (statue about 4.04 m
  against a 1.7 m man — estimate its height by projection). Think &
  Discuss: a triangular plot of 65, 72 and 97 m — can the altitude be
  found? Lesson Assessment printed 61–63, including projection multiple
  choice; x values; a "spot the mistake" item (Magdy against Ibrahim on
  CB); trapezium projections; a 4.5 m ladder (3.6 m up, 2.7 m out) with
  a rope length; a dump-truck hydraulic arm length; and BD with
  AB = AD.
- 3-4 The Circle, printed 64–70. The circle as the set of points at a
  fixed distance from a centre M; radius r, diameter d = 2r, chord,
  circumference and area; drawing a circle with a compass (radius 2 cm);
  a practical activity measuring C/d for a plate, a watch, a coin and a
  CD, giving π ≈ 3.14 (π = 3.141592654…, with 22/7 as the other
  approximation used in the book). Circumference C = πd = 2πr (d = 32
  gives C ≈ 100.5; r = 13.5 gives C = 27π); wheels of 12 cm and 4 cm
  circumference (8 turns of the big wheel give 24 of the small); a car
  wheel of 70 cm circumference (distance per turn; turns for 2.2 km).
  Area derived by cutting a circle into 16 sectors and rearranging them
  into a near-parallelogram: A = πr² (r = 9.2 gives A ≈ 265.9; d = 8
  gives A = 16π; A = 81π gives r = 9 and C ≈ 56.5); a three-quarter
  sector of radius 7 with π = 22/7 (area 115.5, perimeter 47). Get Ready:
  a Ferris wheel (a seat 7 m from the centre). Think & Discuss: a
  football centre circle (how to draw it accurately). Lesson Assessment
  printed 69–70, including multiple choice; circle drawings; C = 18.84
  giving r; A = 78.5 giving C; an inscribed square from C = 75.36; sector
  areas and perimeters; a clock-face area; a coin circumference; a
  wooden board area; a circular rug of diameter 2 m at 150 per square
  metre; bicycle wheels of 42 cm and 14 cm over 66 m (turn counts); and
  a coloured region with a square of side 6.
- 3-5 Right Circular Cylinder and Right Prism, printed 71–78. The right
  circular cylinder: two congruent parallel circular bases, lateral
  surface perpendicular to the bases, height the distance between the
  centres; its net (a 2πr × h rectangle plus two circles); volume
  V = πr²h, lateral area L.A. = 2πrh, total area S.A. = 2πr(h + r).
  Worked examples: r = 4, h = 35 ft (V ≈ 1759.3, L.A. ≈ 879.6,
  S.A. ≈ 980.2); V = 125.6 with h = 2.5 giving r = 4 and S.A. = 163.28.
  The right prism: a polyhedron with two congruent parallel polygon
  bases and rectangular lateral faces, lateral edges perpendicular to
  the bases, named by its base (triangular, quadrilateral, pentagonal);
  V = base area × height, L.A. = base perimeter × height,
  S.A. = L.A. + 2 × base area. Worked examples: a quadrilateral prism
  with base 8 × 4 and S.A. = 280 giving h = 9 and V = 288; a triangular
  prism ingot (base legs 12 and 9, height 20, V = 1080, cut into 3 cm
  cubes giving 40 cubes). Get Ready: a Wadi Degla tent (a triangular
  prism — the cloth area?). Think & Discuss: cups of height 10 and base
  radius 3.5 (the label area per cup). Lesson Assessment printed 76–78,
  including multiple choice; cylinder and prism volumes and areas; a
  pentagonal prism; a cube-equivalent cylinder; a triangular prism with
  base 17, 8 and 12; a cylinder whose radius grows 25%; a pentagonal
  prism of base 105 and height 11 (how many cylinders it makes); a paper
  strip 44 by 8 rolled into a cylinder; a tanker truck 40 ft by 7 ft
  radius; a triangular gift box (base 20, height 10); a water tank
  (radius 0.5 m, height 1.2 m) painted at 210 per square metre; a
  quadrilateral-prism chocolate piece of volume 360; and a creative
  item (a cylinder whose height equals its base circumference, its
  volume in terms of C).
- 3-6 Distance between Two Points in the Coordinate Plane, printed
  79–84. The formula d = √((x₂ − x₁)² + (y₂ − y₁)²), derived via
  Pythagoras with a = |x₂ − x₁| and b = |y₂ − y₁|; the distance from the
  origin d = √(x² + y²); the collinearity test by distances (AB + BC =
  AC means collinear). Worked examples: A(−2, 3) and B(6, −3) give
  d = 10; a distance of 5 between A(1, 7) and B(4, k) gives k = 3 or 11;
  triangle ABC with A(−1, 2), B(13, 2), C(4, 14) has sides 14, 13 and 15
  and perimeter 42; A(−3, 4) is 5 from the origin; two boats from a port
  (A at (7, 6) is √85 ≈ 9.2 nautical miles out, B at (−5, 12) is 13, so A
  is nearer); A(−2, 3), B(0, 4), C(4, 6) are collinear since
  AB = √5, BC = 2√5 and AC = 3√5. A parallelogram proof (A(1, −2),
  B(2, 2), C(−1, 2), D(−2, −2): AB = CD = √17, BC = AD = 3); a circle
  through X(5, k) and Y(−4, 6) with centre M(1, 2) gives k = 7 or −3 and
  area 41π. Get Ready: Sharm El-Sheikh (diver positions A and B on a
  grid; coral-reef distances). Think & Discuss: a football pass from
  (0, 0) to (30, 40). Lesson Assessment printed 83–84, including origin
  distances; AB for (0, 4) and (−3, 0); a "spot the mistake" item (Amal
  against Samar on A(−5, 3) and B(4, 15)); AB lengths; a distance of 2√5
  giving x sets; a circle centred (−3, 5) through (9, −4); a median
  length; collinearity proofs; triangle classification by sides; a
  rectangle proof; a lighthouse beam; football passes on a straight line;
  and a creative item (C the midpoint of AB; AB + OC).
- 3-7 Slope of a Straight Line, printed 85–90. Slope m = Δy/Δx =
  (y₂ − y₁)/(x₂ − x₁); positive, negative, zero and undefined slopes
  with their behaviour (y rising, falling, constant, or x constant as
  the point moves along the line). Worked examples: (1, 2) and (4, 5)
  give m = 1; (−1, 2) and (3, 1) give m = −1/4; (−2, 5) and (1, 5) give
  m = 0; (−2, 1) and (−2, 5) give an undefined slope; slope 3/2 through
  (−1, 5) and (7, k) gives k = 17; a line through (5, k) and (3, 7)
  parallel to the x-axis gives k = 7; collinearity by equal slopes
  (A(0, 1), B(3, 3), C(9, 7) with m = 2/3 throughout). Applications: hill
  against slope steepness (a hill 8/12, m = 2/3, against a slope 6/8,
  m = 3/4 — the slope is steeper); a car's return speed from a
  distance–time graph ((0, 200) to (2.5, 0) gives V = −80 km/h, the
  negative sign meaning the return direction); road gradient as
  rise over run. Get Ready: a wheelchair ramp (the 1:12 maximum slope;
  a 9 m ramp — the height at its end?). Think & Discuss: a distance–
  time graph and a 120 km/h limit (did Samer exceed it?). Lesson
  Assessment printed 89–90, including classifying the slopes of four
  lines; slopes through given point pairs; k for a slope of 1/2 through
  (a, 3) and (2, b); slopes read from graphs; Omar's and Khaled's hills
  (12 m rise over 6 m run against 25 m over 12 m — which is steeper?); a
  plant-growth graph (what the slope means; the height after 15 days);
  and a regular pentagon ABCDEF (the slope of AB).
- Unit Three Assessment, printed 91–92; Unit Three Activity printed 92:
  "Circles in Our Lives" (groups of 4–5; collect circular objects;
  measure; tabulate circumference and area).

#### Unit 4 — Statistics and Probability (opener printed 93)

- 4-1 Samples, printed 94–98. Data collection; primary sources (direct:
  interviews, observation) against secondary sources (institutions and
  their publications, including CAPMAS, the Central Agency for Public
  Mobilization and Statistics); the statistical population; the census
  method (all members — accurate but slow and costly, for example a
  population census or elections) against the sampling method (a
  representative subset — faster and cheaper, but possibly
  unrepresentative); a sample as a small part resembling its population;
  sample types by selection: non-random (biased selection, for example
  asking only trip-goers about trip satisfaction); random selection:
  simple random sample (the lottery-bowl method for small populations;
  the calculator Ran# method for large ones, worked for 300 workers at
  10%, i.e. 30 workers numbered 1–300) and stratified random sample
  (divide the population into strata by trait and sample proportionally:
  a school of 400 girls and 500 boys with a sample of 45 gives 20 girls
  and 25 boys). Get Ready: the Ballon d'Or (the world's best player
  voted by journalists, coaches and captains — a sample of millions of
  fans). Think & Discuss: a favourite-sport survey that asked only the
  basketball team. Lesson Assessment printed 98, including which method
  fits (desert sand; a census; a blood test); a hospital with 40 beds
  and 200 patients, stratified sample 36; a school of 350 pupils, a
  canteen survey at 10% by random sample; a factory (180 men, 120 women,
  80 engineers) with a sample of 57; and 5000 items in four strata, the
  first stratum 1500 with a first-stratum sample of 225 (find the sample
  size).
- 4-2 Probability – Predicting Future Events, printed 99–105. Probability
  as a tool for predicting future events from available information;
  theoretical probability recapped (P(A) = favourable outcomes / total
  outcomes); the expected count as P × total trials. Worked examples: a
  30-pupil sample activities table (football 15, swimming 8, basketball 4,
  table tennis 3) giving P(swimming) = 8/30, an expected 200 swimmers in
  750, P(table tennis) = 3/30 and an expected 75; a phone factory's two
  models over five shops (sales totals 171 and 129 from 300) giving
  P(model 1) = 0.57 and an expected model-1 production of 2850 from 5000;
  an 80-pupil sample by subject preference (Venn diagram: 60 prefer
  mathematics trips, 45 social studies, 35 both) giving P(at least one)
  = 70/80 = 7/8, P(only one subject) = 35/80 = 7/16, an expected 250
  only-social-studies pupils in 2000 and 1125 preferring one subject;
  a visitor survey of 60 (Arab 40, foreign 20, by sex) giving P(male
  foreign) = 8/60 = 2/15 and an expected 300 male foreigners in 2250; a
  sports-day target board (outer radius 15, inner radius 9) giving
  P(hitting the coloured ring) = 144π/225π = 16/25 and an expected 192
  hits in 300. Get Ready: Alexandria weather (the Meteorological
  Authority: rain probability 0.4 in November — the expected rainy days
  in a 30-day month, 12). Think & Discuss: football penalties (3 scored
  from 5; from 20 kicks, an expected 12 goals). Lesson Assessment
  printed 103–105, including a team with win probability 0.75 over 24
  matches (18 expected wins); a factory making 800 a day with defective
  probability 0.03 (24); a wheat yield probability 3/5 with x acres and
  an expected 30 tons (x = 50); a school of 1200 with a sample of 90
  containing 54 girls (720 expected girls); a media team of 18 (4
  teachers, 6 pupils, 8 students); a 5 cm square board with a 2 cm hole;
  a glasses-wearer probability of 0.1 in a class of 40; a sports table;
  a class of 36 (27 pass mathematics, 12 pass Arabic, 9 both); a TV
  factory making 210 a day at two plants; video watching by 60 pupils
  (35 one platform, 25 educational, 10 both); car insurance (3500
  premium, accident probability 0.006, 9000 subscribers); grape exports
  (96,280 tons last year, with potatoes 82,220 and grapes 11,500) against
  a 380,000-ton target; and a virus-test table.
- Unit Four Assessment, printed 106–107; Unit Four Activity printed 107:
  "Prediction List" (groups of 4–5; choose a topic; collect data; predict;
  present a recommendation to the school).
- Final Assessment, printed 108 (a single page, with the print-spec table
  at its foot).

#### Work D — honest negatives

This book does NOT teach: factorisation (Grade 8 Term 1); statistics
tables and graphs (only samples and probability prediction); geometric
transformations (Grade 7 Term 2); powers and roots as a unit (Grade 7
Term 2); solid geometry beyond the cylinder and the prism (no cone,
sphere or pyramid volumes); trigonometry; quadratic functions or their
graphs (only linear functions).

#### Work D — defects and slips

- No missing pages: printed pagination runs continuously 1–108 with every
  contents lesson present at its predicted PDF page.
- The book is image-only, so its text layer is effectively empty apart
  from garbled title-page text in extraction; all content above was read
  from rendered pages.
- The print-spec page count (116) runs above the PDF page count (112);
  the spec evidently counts cover sheets. No content is missing.
- One worked example keeps a negative solution (x, y and 18 in continued
  proportion with x + y = 20 giving (50, −30) alongside (8, 12)); this is
  the book's own working, recorded here, not a defect found by the reader.

### Set-verification resolutions (from the books themselves)

- Grade 7 Term 2 Arabic-only: CONFIRMED. The English and French Grade 7
  files are Term 1 cover to cover (covers, contents and Final Assessment
  at printed 112–113, colophon and back cover); no Term 2 material hides
  in either.
- Grade 8 Term 2 Arabic-only: CONFIRMED on the same evidence (Final
  Assessment at printed 121–122, specification page and blank page; no
  Term 2 material in the English or French files).
- Grade 8 English and French files are the same Term 1 book in different
  languages: CONFIRMED (identical covers and artwork, identical title-
  page team, identical contents at the same printed pages, lesson starts
  at matching PDF pages across all units, identical Final Assessment
  items). SB = Student's Book as printed on the title page; PE2 = Second
  Preparatory; 2PE = Deuxième Préparatoire; T1 = First Term / Premier
  Semestre; "26" = the printed school year 2025–2026. MB is a
  filename/source code only: it appears nowhere in the French book's
  printed text.
- Parallel editions really match section-for-section: Grade 7 contents
  identical in all three languages with sampled French lesson openers at
  the same PDF pages as the English ones and Arabic last pages matching
  the English ones question for question; Grade 8 the same, with French
  lesson starts at matching PDF pages in every unit and a matching Final
  Assessment.

### Record corrections (covers prove these; `records.json` rows 32–39)

1. Grade 8 English row title "Mathematics Preparatory 2 Term 1 (English)"
   is inconsistent with the sibling rows ("Preparatory 7", "Preparatory
   8"). The book itself says "Second Preparatory", i.e. Grade 8. This is
   a naming inconsistency, NOT a misfiling: the grade, term, language
   and kind in the row are all correct. Suggested title: "Mathematics
   Preparatory 8 Term 1 (English)".
2. "Mtah" in the Grade 7 English and French filenames
   (`Mtah_E_Prp1_TR1.pdf`, `Mtah_F_Prp1_TR1.pdf`) is a typo in the
   Egyptian MoE source filename itself: the source blob URLs carry the
   same "Mtah" spelling. The books are Mathematics / Mathématiques. Not
   a record error about the books, but recorded here so nobody "fixes"
   the filename thinking the record misnames the book.
3. Record year "2026" for all eight rows is shorthand for the printed
   school year 2025–2026. Not an error; this draft gives the year as
   printed.
4. No misfiled slots: every file's grade, term, language and kind
   (student) match its record row. Record kind "student" is confirmed for
   all eight: each is a Student's Book. Record publisher (Ministry of
   Education and Technical Education, Egypt) is confirmed for all eight.
5. Nothing in this band is unopened or unverified: all eight PDFs opened,
   passed the `%PDF` check, and were read as described in the Method
   section (Term 1 content in full via the English editions with parallel
   editions spot-checked; Term 2 content page by page visually in full).

### Citation check (2026-11-04, checker — not the draft's author)

Every claim below was tested on the exact page in the real PDF, citing
printed pages via the stated offset. One anchor check = one lesson
opener, lesson-interior page, assessment, activity, contents/colophon/
cover item, or headline test. Offsets verified on far more than three

## Band E — Grades 9–10 (Preparatory 3 and Secondary 1)

Draft read notes for the LiqLearns courses. No repo commit was made; this file is the
only output. All mathematics content below is summarised in my own words; quoted
fragments are short headings or single worked-example lines only.

### Method note (read this first)

- Every PDF was checked for `%PDF` magic and a `pdfinfo` page count before reading.
  All nine files open and are unencrypted.
- Covers and title/imprint pages were read from page images, not from filenames.
  Filenames, record titles and captions were treated as claims to be proved, and
  several of them failed (see "Record corrections" at the end).
- Text-layer books (Grade 9 English and French old editions; Grade 10 Term 2 Arabic
  and the English file misfiled as French) were traced page by page through their
  text layers, with covers, contents pages, seams and lesson starts checked as images.
- Image-only books (both Grade 9 Arabic files; all three Grade 10 Term 1 files) were
  read through covers, title/imprint pages, contents pages, every lesson-start page,
  unit objectives pages, first and last lesson pages, and a mechanical sweep of every
  page (low-resolution render, ink coverage and duplicate hash). The sweep found no
  missing lesson pages in any image-only book; its two findings are reported below.
  Exercise pages inside lessons were swept for presence, not read line by line.
- Printed page numbers below are the numbers printed on the book pages, NOT PDF
  page numbers. PDF page is never equal to printed page in this band (see the
  per-work pagination lines). Where two printings of one work paginate differently,
  both are given and the difference is stated.

### Headline results

- 9 files, 1,319 PDF pages in total.
- 5 distinct works (books). Each combined Grade 9 file holds two works (Term 1 book
  followed by Term 2 book, each with its own pagination restarting at 1).
- The band's traps all resolved from the books themselves:
  - The Grade 9 Arabic "Tr1_2" file is BOTH terms, not Term 2 alone.
  - Grade 9 Term 2 exists in English and French too — inside the same combined
    files, not as separate files.
  - Grade 10 Term 2 exists in English — but the file named French is the English
    book. There is NO French Grade 10 Term 2 file in this band.
  - Grade 10 Term 1 Arabic/English print no 2026-2027 year; the French Term 1 is a
    first edition dated 2025-2026. Same work, different printings.
  - "PDF page = printed page" is false for every book checked in this band.

### Work 1 — Preparatory 3, old edition, Term 1

- Title as printed: "Mathematics — Student's Book, For Preparatory Year three,
  First Term" (English); "Mathématiques — Livre de l'élève, Troisième préparatoire,
  Premier semestre" (French); "الرياضيات — كتاب الطالب، الصف الثالث الإعدادي،
  الفصل الدراسي الأول" (Arabic).
- Grade/term: Grade 9 (Preparatory 3), Term 1.
- Publisher: Arab Republic of Egypt, Ministry of Education and Technical Education,
  Central Administration for Curriculum Development / Central Administration for
  Book Affairs. English title page: Issued 2010, D.N. 8925/2010,
  ISBN 978-977-705-000-5. School year on the covers: 2025-2026.
- Authors (English/French title pages): Mr. Omar Fouad Gaballa,
  Prof. Dr. Afaf Abo-ElFoutoh Saleh, Dr. Essam Wasfy Rouphaiel,
  Mr. Serafiem Elias Skander, Mr. Kamal Yones Kabsha. Revised by the Central
  Administration for Curriculum Development.
- Languages/editions in this band: Arabic, English, French — one work, three
  language editions. The Arabic Term 1 book is the first half of the combined
  Arabic file; the English and French Term 1 books are the first halves of their
  combined files (see Work 2).
- PDF pages: Arabic Term 1 portion PDF pages 1-74 of a 174-page file
  (PDF page 75 is a blank filler carrying only the 2025-2026 footer);
  English Term 1 portion PDF pages 1-73 of a 178-page file; French Term 1 portion
  PDF pages 1-72 of a 176-page file.
- Printed pagination: PDF page is NOT printed page. English: lesson 1-1 is printed
  page 7 on PDF page 8 (offset +1); French: lesson 1-1 is printed page 2 on
  PDF page 8 (offset +6 throughout: lesson 5-4 starts at printed page 64 on
  French PDF page 70, and the last held French Term 1 page is printed page 66
  on PDF page 72). The same exercise page is printed 72 in English and
  printed 66 in French, so the English and French printings paginate differently
  by several pages. Arabic: lesson 1-1 is printed page 2, and the last held
  Arabic Term 1 content page is printed page 67 on PDF page 74.
- How it is taught: each lesson opens with "Think and discuss", then a boxed rule
  under "Learn", worked examples, drills and exercises, ending with a check of
  understanding. Each unit ends with exercises; the books point readers to the
  ministry website for more exercises.

#### Unit-by-unit map (Term 1)

Printed pages are the held French contents-page numbers (French PDF pages 4-5,
read as images). Lesson PDF anchors are from the French file's page trace.
The Arabic contents (PDF pages 5-6, read as images) lists the same 15 lessons
with slightly different numbers (1-1: 2, 1-2: 8, 1-3: 10, 1-4: 13, 2-1: 18,
2-2: 20, 2-3: 26, 3-1: 32, 3-2: 36, 4-1: 44, 4-2: 47, 5-1: 54, 5-2: 57,
5-3: 60, 5-4: 65) and, like the French and English contents, lists no unit
tests, answers, general exercises or exam models: Term 1 ends mid-practice of
lesson 5-4 in all three held printings (English printed 72, French printed 66,
Arabic printed 67), and the Term 2 book starts immediately after.

- Unit 1: Relations and functions (French PDF pages 8-23).
  - 1-1 Cartesian product (contents page 2): ordered pairs, first and second
    projections, arrow diagrams and Cartesian diagrams, the product of two sets
    including R x R, and the fact that order matters.
  - 1-2 Relations (page 8): relations between sets, domain, range, and
    representation.
  - 1-3 Function, also called application (page 11): when a relation is a
    function, its domain, codomain and range, with worked examples.
  - 1-4 Polynomial functions (page 14): constant, linear and quadratic polynomial
    functions and their graphs.
- Unit 2: Ratio and proportion — direct and inverse variation (French PDF pages
  24-37).
  - 2-1 Ratio (page 19): ratio of two quantities, ratio in simplest form, and
    continued ratio.
  - 2-2 Proportion (page 21): proportion, its properties, and mean proportional.
  - 2-3 Direct and inverse variation (page 26): direct variation (y = kx),
    inverse variation (xy = constant), and recognising each from tables and
    problems.
- Unit 3: Statistics (French PDF pages 38-47).
  - 3-1 Collecting data (page 32): populations and samples, sampling by cards
    and other methods, and presenting data.
  - 3-2 Dispersion (page 35): grouped frequency tables with class centres, mean, mean
    deviation in spirit, and the standard deviation computed from squared
    deviations; the worked tables show the x, frequency, x times frequency and
    squared-deviation columns explicitly.
- Unit 4: Trigonometry (French PDF pages 48-56).
  - 4-1 Trigonometric ratios of an acute angle (page 43): sine, cosine and
    tangent from opposite, adjacent and hypotenuse in a right triangle, and the
    identity tan = sin / cos.
  - 4-2 Trigonometric ratios of some angles (page 46): the ratios for 30, 45 and
    60 degrees, and finding an angle when one of its ratios is known.
- Unit 5: Analytic geometry (French PDF pages 57-72).
  - 5-1 Distance between two points (page 52): the distance formula and its use.
  - 5-2 Coordinates of the midpoint of a segment (page 56): the midpoint formula, used to
    find a midpoint and to work backwards to an endpoint.
  - 5-3 Slope of a straight line (page 59): slope as the tangent of
    the positive angle with the x-axis, slopes of parallel lines (equal) and of
    perpendicular lines (product -1).
  - 5-4 The equation of a straight line given its slope and its y-intercept
    (page 64): y = mx + c, finding the equation through a vertex and a
    midpoint, and writing it in general form.

#### What this book does NOT teach

No equations systems, no algebraic fractions, no probability, no circle geometry,
no matrices, no vectors, no complex numbers, no calculus. Similarity and dilation
are absent here (they belong to the new Grade 9 book and to Grade 10 Term 1).
Statistics stops at dispersion; there is no correlation or regression.

#### Held-copy defects and slips (Term 1)

- Term 1 ends mid-practice of lesson 5-4 in all three held printings (English:
  last content page printed 72 on PDF page 73; French: printed 66 on PDF page
  72; Arabic: printed 67 on PDF page 74) and the Term 2 book starts immediately
  after (English: blank divider page "Second Term" at PDF page 74; French: Term 2
  cover at PDF page 73; Arabic: Term 2 divider at PDF page 76). No back matter
  is listed in any held contents page, and none is present: lesson sequence 1-1
  to 5-4 is itself continuous, so no lesson is missing — the books simply stop
  there. (An earlier version of this draft wrongly claimed the French contents
  listed unit tests, answers on page 85, general exercises on page 86 and exam
  models on page 99, with lesson 5-4 at contents page 79; those numbers appear
  in no held contents page and have been removed.)
- The English file's text layer is partly garbled: several pages carry doubled
  text and a shifted alphabet (a Caesar-like shift, e.g. the Term 2 opener's
  figure caption), and symbol mojibake on rotated pages. The page images read
  cleanly; only the text layer is affected.
- The English Term 1 contents pages carry Sahara Printing Company furniture
  naming 2018-2019 in the text layer, against a 2025-2026 title page — a sign
  that the contents pages were reused from an earlier printing. Flagged for the
  merger; the lesson content itself matches the French 2025-2026 printing.

### Work 2 — Preparatory 3, old edition, Term 2

- Title as printed: "Student's Book — Second Term" (English divider page);
  "Livre de l'élève — Deuxième semestre" (French Term 2 cover and lesson
  footers); "الفصل الدراسي الثاني" (Arabic Term 2 divider).
- Grade/term: Grade 9 (Preparatory 3), Term 2. Same authors and ministry imprint
  as Work 1; same school year 2025-2026.
- Languages/editions in this band: Arabic, English, French — one work, three
  language editions, each held only as the second half of its combined file.
  There is no separate Term 2 file in any language in this band.
- PDF pages: Arabic Term 2 portion approx. PDF pages 76-174 of the 174-page file
  (divider page 76; lessons from about page 80); English Term 2 portion PDF pages
  74-178 (divider page 74, contents pages 75-76, lessons pages 77-176, technical
  page 177, back matter page 178); French Term 2 portion PDF pages 73-176 (cover
  page 73, divider page 76, lessons pages 77-175, revision credits page 176).
- Printed pagination: Term 2 pagination restarts at 1. English contents-page
  numbers are used below. PDF page is NOT printed page: English contents at
  PDF pages 75-76 serve lessons from PDF page 77 on; French Term 2 lesson pages
  run about 4 pages earlier than the English contents numbers (French lesson 4-1
  is printed page 35, English contents says 39; French lesson 5-1 is printed
  page 60, English contents says 64). Arabic Term 2 lesson 2-1 (set of zeros of
  a polynomial function) is printed pages 13-14 on PDF pages 90-91.
- How it is taught: same pattern as Term 1 (think and discuss, boxed rule,
  examples, drills, check of understanding), with unit summaries and general
  exercises at the end of each unit in the Term 2 book.

#### Unit-by-unit map (Term 2)

Printed pages are the English Term 2 contents-page numbers.

- Unit 1: Equations.
  - 1-1 Solving two equations of the first degree in two variables, graphically
    and algebraically (page 5): the graphical intersection and the algebraic
    methods, including the quadratic-function figure that opens the unit.
  - 1-2 Solving an equation of the second degree in one unknown, graphically
    and algebraically (page 10): roots from the graph and by formula/factorising.
  - 1-3 Solving two equations in two variables, one of the first degree and the
    other of the second degree (page 13).
- Unit 2: Algebraic fractional functions and the operations on them.
  - 2-1 Set of zeros of a polynomial function (page 15): values that make the
    function zero, and using zeros to solve equations.
  - 2-2 Algebraic fractional function (page 17): a ratio of two polynomials,
    its domain as all real numbers except the zeros of the denominator.
  - 2-3 Equality of two algebraic fractions (page 20): when two fractions are
    equal, with domain conditions.
  - 2-4 Operations on algebraic fractions (page 24): addition, subtraction,
    multiplication and division, with factorising and cancelling.
- Unit 3: Probability.
  - 3-1 Operations on events (page 30): union, intersection and related
    operations on events.
  - 3-2 Complementary event and the difference between two events (page 35).
- Unit 4: The circle.
  - 4-1 Basic definitions and concepts (page 39): centre, radius, diameter,
    chord, secant, tangent and arc.
  - 4-2 Positions of a point, a straight line and a circle with respect to a
    circle (page 46): inside, on, outside; intersecting, tangent, separate.
  - 4-3 Identifying the circle (page 54): determining a circle from given
    conditions.
  - 4-4 The relation between the chords of a circle and its centre (page 58):
    equal chords and their distances from the centre.
- Unit 5: Angles and arcs in the circle.
  - 5-1 Central angle and measuring arcs (page 64).
  - 5-2 The relation between the inscribed and central angles subtended by the
    same arc (page 71).
  - 5-3 Inscribed angles subtended by the same arc (page 79).
  - 5-4 Cyclic quadrilaterals (page 85).
  - 5-5 Properties of cyclic quadrilaterals (page 88).
  - 5-6 The relation between the tangents of a circle (page 93).
  - 5-7 Angle of tangency (page 100).

#### What this book does NOT teach

No matrices, vectors or complex numbers; no similarity, dilation or analytic
geometry beyond the circle; no statistics (that was Term 1); no trigonometry
beyond what the circle work assumes; no calculus. Probability is limited to
operations on events and complements — no probability distributions, no
counting methods taught as a separate topic.

#### Held-copy defects and slips (Term 2)

- Arabic file: two effectively blank filler pages, PDF pages 75 (end of Term 1)
  and 79 (inside the Term 2 front matter), each carrying only the 2025-2026
  footer. No content is lost; the lesson sequence is continuous around them.
- The English file ends with an Arabic technical-specifications page (PDF page
  177) stating 180 pages, and a final page (178) carrying an insurance-fund box.
  The French file ends with a revision-credits page (PDF page 176) naming
  Prof. Manal Azkoul, Mr. Akram Fawzy and Mr. Rachad Farag. Neither file has an
  answers section after Term 2.

### Work 3 — Preparatory 3, NEW edition, Term 1 (2026-2027, GPS)

- Title as printed: "الرياضيات — كتاب الطالب، للصف الثالث الإعدادي، الفصل
  الدراسي الأول" (Mathematics — Student's Book, Third Preparatory, First
  Semester). Lesson titles carry English subtitles (e.g. "Powers of Powers",
  "Quadratic Function", "Dilation").
- Grade/term: Grade 9 (Preparatory 3), Term 1 only. No Term 2 of this new edition
  is in this band.
- Publisher: Ministry of Education and Technical Education (copyright line,
  2026/2027); prepared and published by GPS Company for Printing, Publishing and
  Distribution. General supervision: Dr. Gabriel Anwar Hamida, head of the Central
  Administration for Curriculum Development. New author team headed by Osama
  Zaid Sharif (authors page, PDF page 3) — a different team from the old edition.
- Language: Arabic only in this band. No English or French edition of the new
  book is held here.
- Edition/year as printed: 2026-2027 (cover title page, PDF page 2). Imprint
  page (PDF page 113): size 19 x 27 cm, 116 stated pages, deposit number
  2026/14803 as read, ISBN 978-633-8530-22-8.
- PDF pages: 114 (cover PDF page 1, title PDF page 2, authors PDF page 3,
  introduction printed page iii, contents printed page iv, lessons printed
  pages 2-107, final assessment printed page 107, imprint PDF page 113, back
  cover PDF page 114).
- Printed pagination: PDF page is NOT printed page. Lesson 1-1 is printed page 2
  on PDF page 7 (offset +5 throughout: printed page = PDF page minus 5).
  The imprint's "116 pages" against 114 PDF pages is a 2-page counting
  difference (covers/imprint), not a content gap: all 15 contents lessons were
  verified at their lesson-start pages, and the book closes with a Unit 4
  activity (printed page 106) and a final assessment (printed page 107).
- How it is taught: a new pedagogy, different from the old edition. Every lesson
  has a right-hand sidebar with learning outcomes, bilingual vocabulary
  (Arabic term with its English equivalent) and prior knowledge; the body runs
  "Get ready" (a real-life hook: an Alexandria aquarium tank, a dolphin show, a
  shadow and a palm tree, a kite, an obelisk, fighter jets, a Luxor souvenir
  market stall), then "Think and discuss", then "Learn" with the rule boxed, then
  worked examples and exercises. Each unit closes with an activity; the book
  closes with a final assessment and a self-evaluation strip.

#### Unit-by-unit map (new Term 1)

Printed pages are the contents-page numbers; every lesson start was verified
on its page image.

- Unit 1: Numbers and operations on them.
  - 1-1 Powers of powers (page 2): (a to the m) to the n equals a to the m times
    n, with the earlier exponent laws recapped in the prior-knowledge box
    (multiplying, dividing, power of a product, power of a quotient, negative
    exponent, zero exponent).
  - 1-2 Exponential equations (page 7): equations with the unknown in the
    exponent, of the form a to the x equals b, solved by writing both sides
    with equal bases; the bacterial-doubling hook opens the lesson.
- Unit 2: Algebra.
  - 2-1 Quadratic function (page 14): standard form f(x) = ax squared + bx + c,
    the parabola, its axis of symmetry x = -b / 2a, vertex, y-intercept and
    maximum/minimum value, with graphing by table.
  - 2-2 Set of real zeros of polynomial functions (page 22): polynomial
    functions by degree (linear, quadratic, cubic), what a zero is, and finding
    the real zeros, including cases with no real zero.
  - 2-3 Rational algebraic functions (page 27): a ratio of two polynomials,
    its domain excluding denominator zeros, and its zeros.
  - 2-4 Common domain and equality of rational algebraic functions (page 33):
    the common domain as the intersection of domains, and when two rational
    functions are equal.
  - 2-5 Operations on rational algebraic fractions (page 38): addition and
    subtraction by factorising denominators and taking a common denominator,
    multiplication, division and reciprocals, always in simplest form.
- Unit 3: Geometry.
  - 3-1 Similarity of polygons (page 48): the two conditions (corresponding
    angles equal, corresponding sides proportional) and the similarity ratio.
  - 3-2 Similarity of triangles (page 55): similarity cases including AA, and
    using similarity to find unknown lengths (the Thales/shadow idea returns
    here in new form).
  - 3-3 Dilation (page 62): centre of dilation, dilation factor k, enlargement
    for k greater than 1, reduction for k between 0 and 1, coordinates of the
    image, and the fact that a figure and its image are similar with unchanged
    angle measures.
  - 3-4 Trigonometric ratios of an acute angle (page 68): sine, cosine and
    tangent from a right triangle, with tan = sin / cos.
  - 3-5 Trigonometric ratios of some special angles (page 74): the table for
    30, 45 and 60 degrees, derived from the 30-60-90 and 45-45-90 triangles.
  - 3-6 The relation between the slopes of two parallel and perpendicular
    straight lines (page 80): slope m = tan of the inclination angle, equal
    slopes for parallels, product -1 for perpendiculars.
  - 3-7 Equation of the straight line (page 86): y = mx + b from slope and
    y-intercept, and other forms including through a point with a given slope.
- Unit 4: Statistics.
  - 4-1 Measures of dispersion (page 96): why central-tendency measures alone
    are not enough; range, interquartile range, mean deviation and standard
    deviation, worked from data sets and from a frequency table, with a group
    activity (page 106) applying central tendency and dispersion to real
    collected data, and a final assessment (page 107) covering the whole term.

#### What this book does NOT teach

This is a restructured Term 1, not the old Term 1 with a new cover. It does NOT
teach Cartesian products, relations or functions as mappings (old Term 1 Unit 1
is gone); it does NOT teach ratio, proportion or variation; it does NOT teach
data collection methods; it does NOT teach distance between two points or the
midpoint formula; it does NOT teach probability, circle geometry, equation
systems, matrices, vectors or complex numbers. Note the moves: old Term 2
algebra (quadratic function, rational functions) has moved into this Term 1,
and old Term 1 trigonometry and line-equation material has moved into Unit 3
alongside similarity and dilation (which previously lived in Grade 10 Term 1).

#### Held-copy defects and slips (new Term 1)

- None found. The mechanical sweep found no blank pages and no duplicated pages.
  One reading note: the contents page lists only lessons; the Unit 4 activity
  and the final assessment are not listed in the contents but are present at
  printed pages 106-107.

  single-page cover; its last two pages repeat those spreads (see defects).
- Printed pagination: PDF page is NOT printed page. English: lesson 1-1 is
  printed page 4 on PDF page 8 (offset +4); the last lesson page is printed
  page 119 on PDF page 123. Arabic shares the English printed pagination
  (last lesson page printed 119 on PDF page 121, offset +2). French contents
  numbers run later than English
  (lesson 1-2: French 11, English 10; lesson 4-6: French 119, English 116; last
  lesson page French printed 122 on PDF page 128, offset +6 throughout).
- How it is taught: same old-edition pattern — "Think and discuss" or group
  work, "Learn" with boxed definitions and theorems (several theorems are
  explicitly marked "proof is not required"), worked examples, "Try to solve",
  exercises and a check of understanding; each unit opens with objectives, key
  terms and a brief history (al-Khwarizmi for Unit 1, Thales for Unit 2).

#### Unit-by-unit map (Secondary 1, Term 1)

Printed pages are the English contents-page numbers; French contents numbers
are given in brackets where they differ.

- Unit 1: Algebra, relations and functions.
  - 1-1 An introduction in complex numbers (page 4; French 4): the imaginary
    unit i with i squared = -1, imaginary numbers, the complex number in
    algebraic form a + bi, powers of i, equality of two complex numbers, and
    operations on complex numbers. Motivated by x squared = -1 having no real
    solution and by the graph of y = x squared + 1 never meeting the x-axis.
  - 1-2 Determining the types of the two roots of a quadratic equation
    (page 10; French 11): the discriminant b squared - 4ac; two distinct real
    roots, two equal real roots, or no real roots.
  - 1-3 The relation between the two roots of the second degree equation and
    the coefficients of its terms (page 14; French 16): sum of roots = -b/a,
    product = c/a, and forming a quadratic equation from given roots or from
    another equation's roots.
  - 1-4 Sign of a function (page 20; French 22): where a constant, linear or
    quadratic function is positive, negative or zero, read from its graph.
  - 1-5 Quadratic inequalities in one variable (page 27; French 29): solving
    by the graph of the related quadratic function and writing the solution
    set as intervals.
- Unit 2: Similarity.
  - 2-1 Similarity of polygons (page 32; French 34): definition (same shape,
    corresponding angles congruent, corresponding sides proportional),
    similarity ratio, perimeters and drawing scale.
  - 2-2 Similarity of triangles (page 36; French 38): similarity cases, the
    Thales pyramid-height story, and the properties of the perpendicular from
    the right angle to the hypotenuse.
  - 2-3 The relation between the areas of two similar polygons (page 45;
    French 48): area ratio equals the square of the similarity ratio, proved
    first for triangles.
  - 2-4 Applications of similarity in the circle (page 53; French 56):
    intersecting chords (EA x EB = EC x ED), secants from an outside point,
    and tangent-secant relations, all deduced from similar triangles.
- Unit 3: The triangle proportionality theorems.
  - 3-1 Parallel lines and proportional parts (page 62; French 66): a line
    parallel to one side of a triangle divides the other two sides
    proportionally, with proof via similar triangles.
  - 3-2 Angle bisectors and proportional parts (page 72; French 76): the
    internal and external bisectors divide the opposite side in the ratio of
    the other two sides (proof not required).
- Unit 4: Trigonometry.
  - 4-1 Directed angle (page 82; French 86): initial and terminal sides,
    standard position, positive and negative measure, equivalent angles,
    quadrantal angles, and degree-minute-second measure.
  - 4-2 Systems of measuring angle, degree and radian (page 90; French 94):
    radian measure as arc length over radius, arc length l = theta x r, and
    conversion between degrees and radians.
  - 4-3 Trigonometric functions (page 95; French 98): sine, cosine, tangent
    and their reciprocals sec, csc, cot from the unit circle, their signs by
    quadrant, and values for special angles.
  - 4-4 Related angles (page 103; French 106): functions of 180 - theta,
    180 + theta, 360 - theta and 90 - theta, and the general solution of
    simple trigonometric equations in the form given.
  - 4-5 Graphing trigonometric functions (page 112; French 115): tabulating
    and drawing the sine and cosine curves, maximum and minimum values.
  - 4-6 Finding the measure of an angle given the value of one of its
    trigonometric ratios (page 116; French 119): inverse notation (sin to the
    -1 and the like), calculator key sequences, and all solutions between
    0 and 360 degrees, ending with applied problems including a ladder
    problem in radians.

#### What this book does NOT teach

No matrices, vectors or linear programming (those are Term 2); no probability
or statistics; no trigonometric identities or equations as a separate topic
(those are Term 2 Unit 5); no calculus; no three-dimensional geometry. Radian
measure and graphing of trig functions live here in Term 1, not in Term 2.

  repeated after the final lesson page, printed 122 at PDF page 128). No lesson
  content is lost; the file simply ends with two repeated cover pages.
- English book prints "ISPN" where ISBN is meant, on its title page.
- The record years "2026_2027" for the Arabic and English files are not printed
  in the books examined (English imprint: Issued 2013). Treat 2026_2027 as a
  source or release year, not as the edition year. The French file's printed
  year is "first edition 2025-2026".
- Mechanical sweep of all three files found no blank pages and (apart from the
  French repeated covers) no duplicated pages.

### Work 5 — Secondary 1, old edition, Term 2

- Title as printed: "Mathematics — Student's Book, First form secondary, SECOND
  TERM" (English cover, 2025-2026); "الرياضيات — الصف الأول الثانوي، الفصل
  الدراسي الثاني" (Arabic cover, 2025-2026, same Gaballa author team).
- Grade/term: Grade 10 (Secondary 1), Term 2.
- Publisher: Ministry of Education, Book Sector (English title page). English
  title page carries the year 2025-2026 only (read as an image; no 2016-2017
  year appears on the English title page).
- Languages/editions in this band: Arabic and ENGLISH — not French. The file
  named French in this band is the English book (see the record correction
  below). No French Term 2 file is held in this band.
- PDF pages: Arabic 150, English (misfiled as French) 153. The English file
  ends at PDF page 153 with Unit 5 general exercises; there is no blank
  trailing page in the PDF (an empty "page 154" reported by one text
  extraction is an artefact, not a PDF page).
- Printed pagination: PDF page is NOT printed page. Arabic: lesson 1-1 is
  printed page 4 on PDF page 6 (offset +2 throughout, verified at PDF page 6
  to printed 4, PDF page 109 to printed 107, PDF page 113 to printed 111,
  PDF page 117 to printed 115, PDF page 120 to printed 118 and PDF page 149
  to printed 147). English: footer "Student book — Second term" with printed
  page 90 on PDF page 95 (offset +5, verified at PDF pages 95, 101, 105 and
  108 to printed pages 90, 96, 100 and 103). The English contents page carries
  doubled, rotated numerals in extraction, so its printed starts below are
  given as read from the contents and cross-checked against body lesson and
  exercise pages; the two printings' printed paginations diverge in the later
  units (see the Unit 3 and Unit 4 notes).
- How it is taught: same old-edition pattern — unit objectives and key terms
  (Arabic and English side by side in the Arabic book), "You will learn",
  "Think and discuss", "Learn", worked examples, "Try to solve", exercises,
  a unit summary and general exercises at the end of every unit. Tools named
  include the scientific calculator, spreadsheet programs and a computer.

#### Unit-by-unit map (Secondary 1, Term 2)

Printed starts are the English contents-page numbers; Arabic body anchors
(PDF pages) are given where they pin a lesson down.

- Unit 1: Algebra — Matrices.
  - 1-1 Organizing data in matrices (contents page 4; Arabic PDF page 6):
    rows, columns, order m x n, representing factory-production data as a
    matrix, and special matrices (row, column, square, zero, diagonal, unit,
    symmetric, skew-symmetric), transpose and equality of matrices.
  - 1-2 Adding and subtracting matrices (contents page 14; Arabic PDF page 17):
    addition, subtraction and multiplication by a real number, with their
    properties.
  - 1-3 Multiplying matrices (contents page 18; Arabic PDF page 21):
    matrix multiplication, when it is defined, and its properties.
  - 1-4 Determinants (contents page 22; Arabic PDF page 30): determinants of
    order 2 x 2 and 3 x 3, triangular determinants, and the area of a triangle
    from a determinant.
  - 1-5 Multiplicative inverse of a matrix (contents page 30; Arabic PDF page
    39): inverse of a 2 x 2 square matrix, solving two simultaneous equations
    by inverse matrix and by Cramer's rule.
  - Unit summary (contents page 35) and general exercises close the unit
    (Arabic PDF pages 44-46).
- Unit 2: Algebra — Linear programming.
  - 2-1 Linear inequalities (contents page 38; Arabic PDF page 48 — PDF page
    47 is the Unit 2 opener): linear inequalities in one
    and two variables and their solution sets.
  - 2-2 Solving systems of linear inequalities graphically (contents page 43;
    Arabic PDF page 53): graphing each inequality and shading the common region.
  - 2-3 Linear programming and optimization (contents page 48; Arabic PDF
    page 59): the objective function, feasible region, and greatest/least values
    at corner points, with applied buying and production problems.
  - General exercises close the unit (Arabic PDF pages 64-66).
- Unit 3: Analytic geometry — Vectors.
  - 3-1 Scalars, vectors and directed line segments (contents page 58; Arabic
    PDF page 68 — PDF page 67 is the Unit 3 opener): scalar versus vector
    quantities, directed segments, and position vectors.
  - 3-2 Vectors (contents page 63; Arabic PDF page 75): vectors in terms of the
    unit vectors, magnitude (modulus), and equality of vectors.
  - 3-3 Operations on vectors (contents page 71; Arabic PDF page 85): addition
    (including the parallelogram rule), subtraction and scalar multiplication.
  - Note: the contents page also lists a lesson 3-4, "Applications on vectors".
    No separate 3-4 lesson exists in either body: the Arabic Unit 3 opener
    lists only three lessons, and the English body runs from Exercise 3-3
    (printed page 86) straight into Unit 4, while the Arabic body runs from
    lesson 3-3 into the Unit 3 general exercises (Arabic PDF pages 92-94).
    Vector proofs and coordinate-plane problems appear inside lesson 3-3 and
    in those general exercises, not as a 3-4 lesson.
- Unit 4: Analytic geometry — Straight line.
  - 4-1 Division of a line segment (contents page 86; Arabic PDF page 96 —
    PDF page 95 is the Unit 4 opener): internal division,
    midpoint as a special case, and external division.
  - 4-2 Equation of a straight line (contents page 91; Arabic PDF page 101):
    equation from a point and a direction vector, from two points, and from
    slope and intercept, with conversions between forms.
  - 4-3 Measure of the angle between two straight lines (contents page 96;
    Arabic PDF page 109): angle from slopes and direction vectors, parallel
    and perpendicular cases. (Arabic body only: the English body has no
    separate 4-3 lesson — see the 4-5 note.)
  - 4-4 Length of the perpendicular from a point to a straight line (contents
    page 98; Arabic PDF page 113): the distance formula and problems including
    distance between parallels. (Arabic body only, as for 4-3.)
  - Unit summary and general exercises (Arabic PDF pages 117-118).
  - Note: the contents page also lists a lesson 4-5, "General equation of a
    straight line passing through the point of intersection of two lines".
    No separate 4-5 lesson exists in either the Arabic or the English body.
    In Arabic, lesson 4-4 exercises run straight into the Unit 4 general
    exercises (PDF pages 117-118); the Arabic Unit 4 opener itself lists only
    four lessons (4-1 to 4-4). In English the gap is wider: the body runs
    from lesson 4-2 (equation of a straight line, seen in the body at printed
    page 100) and its exercises (printed pages 103-105) straight into Unit 5 at printed
    page 106, so lessons 4-3 and 4-4 likewise have no separate lesson in the
    English body, and the contents numbers for Unit 4 (4-1: 86, 4-2: 91,
    4-3: 96, 4-4: 98, 4-5: 100, summary: 103) do not match the English body.
    The 4-5 topic is covered inside the straight-line equation material, not
    as its own lesson.
- Unit 5: Trigonometry.
  - 5-1 Trigonometric identities (Arabic PDF page 120): the basic identities
    and simplifying expressions with them.
  - 5-2 Solving trigonometric equations (Arabic PDF page 126): equations in
    sin, cos and tan over a given interval.
  - 5-3 Solving the right-angled triangle (Arabic PDF page 131): finding
    unknown sides and angles from given data.
  - 5-4 Angles of elevation and angles of depression (Arabic PDF page 133):
    applied problems including a rock-and-ships problem and a circle-sector
    problem.
  - 5-5 Circular sector (Arabic PDF page 137): sector area from angle and
    radius.
  - 5-6 Circular segment (Arabic PDF page 141): segment area as sector minus
    triangle.
  - 5-7 Areas (Arabic PDF page 147): area of a triangle from two sides and the
    included angle, and area of a regular polygon.
  - General exercises close the unit and the book (Arabic PDF pages 149-150;
    English PDF pages 152-153).

#### What this book does NOT teach

No complex numbers, similarity or triangle-proportionality theorems (Term 1);
no probability or statistics; no graphing of trigonometric functions and no
radian measure as a topic (Term 1 Unit 4); no calculus; no three-dimensional
geometry. Trigonometry here is identities, equations, right-triangle solving
and circle mensuration only.

#### Held-copy defects and slips (Term 2)

- The contents-listed lesson 4-5 has no separate lesson in the body (see above);
  the same holds for lesson 3-4 and, in the English body, lessons 4-3 and 4-4.
- The Arabic text layer joins letters incorrectly in extraction (broken Arabic
  shaping), and the English text layer doubles some pages' text; page images
  read cleanly in both files.
- The book itself ends with Unit 5 general exercises, with no final exam
  section (an empty "page 154" reported by one English text extraction is an
  artefact; the PDF has 153 pages).
